h  o      r  i  c  e  u  to     la        r  i  s  p  o  st a
… the opening of the despatch of 4 August 1629 under the 1628 key of Biermann and Bosbach; 1 and 8 are nulls

Vienna → Rome · homophonic cipher with nomenclator and nulls · August–November 1629

Pallotto to Barberini, 1629

→

Twenty-eight ciphered passages in the register of Monsignor Giovanni Battista Pallotto, archbishop of Thessalonica and nuncio to the Emperor: Vatican City, BAV, Barb.lat. 6960, digitised in full on DigiVatLib. DECODE carries them as R286–R313, all “Non-decrypted” (no decipherment on the record), and the catalogue entry said that what the cipher hides is not known.

The contents are known. Hans Kiewning printed these despatches in 1897 from the Roman office’s own decipherments, and the register’s clear pages confirm the identification word for word. The key is known too: Norbert Biermann and Thomas Bosbach recovered it in 2018 for the sibling volume Barb.lat. 6956, and George Lasry had found that 6960 uses it. Applied here to DECODE’s transcriptions, it gives Italian words in every record, but with garbled strings and about 1,750 three-digit groups missing from the 6956 key, the automatic decode does not build sentences. A second decode (22 October 2026), every sheet aligned with Kiewning’s print and 125 code groups learnt from it, brings 22 of the 28 sheets to readings with English translations (one of them, R303, at 94.3% of its words, under the 95% a complete reading needs); six are still decoded, not read.

Daniel Bourdeau · posted 20 September 2026 · updated 26 October 2026 · key identified 21 September 2026

Method · Sheets Kiewning prints verbatim (R286, R287, R289, R306, R307, R311, R312; parts of R288, R290, R292-R299, R302, R305, R310): read from existing decipherment (complete) · Passages Kiewning gives only as German regest (most of R288, R291-R305, R308-R310, R313): read with known key (partial) · On the map

Credits · Norbert Biermann (key or decipherment used) · Thomas Bosbach (key or decipherment used) · George Lasry (pointer to a source) · Hans Kiewning (earlier solution)

At a glanceKnown key applied · complete

The document
Barb.lat. 6960, the register of Pallotto’s nunciature at the imperial court, 1629: 194 pages mixing all-figure sheets with clear Italian.
Where
Vatican, Biblioteca Apostolica Vaticana, Barb.lat. 6960; DECODE R286–R313 hold 28 ciphered passages, catalogued as “Non-decrypted”.
The cipher
Homophonic: two-digit codes for letters, syllables and short words, three-digit nomenclator groups (200–999) for names, and 1 and 8 as nulls.
How it was read
The key of the sibling volume 6956 (Biermann and Bosbach, 2018), which George Lasry reported also fits 6960. Applied to all 28 records it gives running Italian.
What it says
Despatches of August–September 1629 on the Mantuan succession, printed by Kiewning in 1897. R286 opens ho riceuto la risposta datami in scritto.
Still open
About 1,750 three-digit groups are not in the 6956 key (presumably 1629 names and titles). Some 200 digits fit no code.
Credit
Norbert Biermann and Thomas Bosbach (key); George Lasry (the link to 6960).
Summary (20 September 2026). Barb.lat. 6960 is the Barberini register of Pallotto’s nunciature at the imperial court in the second half of 1629, the months when the Mantuan succession was pushing the Emperor and France towards war. Its 194 pages interleave sheets written entirely in figures with pages of clear Italian. DECODE catalogued twenty-eight of the ciphered sheets with no decipherment and no indication of their contents. Their contents have been published since 1897: Hans Kiewning’s volume of the Nuntiaturberichte aus Deutschland for 1629 prints some forty of Pallotto’s despatches under the heading dechiffr., taken from the copies received and deciphered in Rome, now ASV, Nunz. di Germania 119. The first ciphered sheet, pp. 5–6, is headed in clear Di Vienna 4 di Agosto and is Kiewning’s Nr. 153; the register’s clear pages on either side of it run straight through the cipher sheet and carry that document’s wording exactly, and p. 13 is its second enclosure, again verbatim. So the contents were known; the key, on 20 September, was not. The cipher is numeric and written without separation, and its code width could not be established: there is no phase preference for two-digit or three-digit codes anywhere in the 112,805 digits. The opening twenty-seven letters of the 4 August despatch do align as two-digit codes, and then the alignment breaks for good. A control shows that twenty-seven is below the chance baseline for this material, so that match is not evidence of anything. No partial key is claimed: a held-out test shows that the table learned from one part of the despatch predicts the rest no better than the same table with its letters shuffled (0.355 confirmations per letter against a control mean of 0.359), so the 55 per cent consistency an aligner reports is the aligner fitting the crib. What the failure does have is a measured cause. On synthetic ciphertext (the same plaintext under a random two-digit key) the identical pipeline recovers the whole key from a clean transcription, 87 codes of 100 at a 0.5 per cent digit error rate, and 7 of 100 at 1.7 per cent, because each inserted or dropped digit flips the code phase for everything after it. So the method works. A third test then went further: the longest provably consistent two-digit alignment between the cipher and the known plaintext is 39 letters, and controls that shuffle the plaintext or reverse the cipher give 36 to 39, while the synthetic cipher at the same noise gives 207. The real material sits exactly on its chance baseline. Transcription noise is therefore not a sufficient explanation. A fourth test then searched the whole printed volume (1,161,303 letters) for the plaintext of any window of four of the ciphertexts, at code widths two, three and four, by a method calibrated to find a planted encipherment in 16 windows out of 254 even at the real noise level, with no false positives. It found nothing. So these ciphertexts are not fixed-width substitutions of any text printed in the edition. One test came back positive: the digit stream holds thirty-nine repeated twenty-four-digit strings where chance gives none, which a carefully used homophonic cipher would not produce. This pointed to a nomenclator writing each word or syllable the same way every time, in groups of varying length. A late find: DECODE’s own transcriptions carry some 640 clear-text annotations, and in five records a decipherment written on the page itself. Most of these despatches are mixed letters, short ciphered clauses set in clear Italian, which is why a crib drawn from the printed edition could not match them. That is where the next attempt should start. Status on 20 September, superseded by the update above: contents already in print; key not recovered, no partial key claimed, and every fixed-width hypothesis ruled out by calibrated controls. Two possibilities remained: a variable-length nomenclator, or ciphered passages whose Italian is nowhere in print because the edition summarises them in German.

00 The key, found by others

The sibling volume, Barb.lat. 6956, holds seventy ciphered sheets of the same nunciature for May to August 1628 (DECODE R215–R285 and R318). Its key was recovered in March 2018 by Norbert Biermann and, independently, Thomas Bosbach. Their two keys, a merged key and a sample decryption are filed on DECODE as documents attached to the first ciphertext record, R215, not as a key record. That is why this project's metadata harvest never saw them. DECODE still lists all twenty-eight 6960 records as “Non-decrypted”.

The system:

  • Two-digit homophones for the letters: a = 00, 02, 20; e = 09, 30, 90; i = 05, 40, 50; o = 03, 07, 70; and so on.
  • Two-digit groups for syllables and short words: 47 che, 52 con, 57 per, 69 di, 73 la, 93 to.
  • Three-digit nomenclator groups from 200 to 999 for names and titles: 360 Duca di Mantova, 460 Imperatore, 670 Sua Maestà, 925 Spagna.
  • The single digits 1 and 8 as nulls, mostly at word boundaries.
  • Doubled letters are often written once, so 33 can stand for d or dd.

Applied to DECODE’s transcriptions of all twenty-eight 6960 records, dividing the digit stream into one-, two- and three-digit units by dynamic programming, the key gives Italian words in every record. R286 opens ho riceuto la risposta datami in scritto, the first words of Kiewning’s Nr. 153. About 2,000 nomenclator groups resolve from the 6956 key. About 1,750 three-digit groups do not appear in it; they are presumably names and titles particular to 1629, and they stay open. Some 200 digits fit no code, which is consistent with slips in the volunteer transcription. The unedited output is in the repository (targets/pallotto1629/key6956/decrypt_6960.txt). Read as text it has garbled strings or open groups in most clauses, so it is not a reading: the reading audit of 22 October 2026 kept only R286, through Kiewning’s print.

The re-reading of 22 October 2026. A better decode (research/reading_audit/reread_b01_decode.py) keeps the nulls 1 and 8 out of the groups (no key group contains them, and the first decoder let unknown groups swallow them), chooses the two- and three-digit division with an Italian 5-gram model, keeps DECODE’s uncertain digits as alternatives, and models DECODE’s frequent misreading of this hand’s null 8 as 0, seen on the scans. Every sheet was then matched to Kiewning and aligned with the print chunk by chunk. Kiewning prints R286, R287, R289, R306, R307, R311 and R312 verbatim and parts of most others; the rest he gives as German regest, and there the reading rests on the figures, checked on the DECODE scans. The alignment taught 125 nomenclator groups missing from the 6956 key (for example 577 N. S., 639 re di Francia, 290 Cardinal di Richelieu, 595 principe d’Echembergh, 440 Gesuiti) and corrected three (776 is tutto, not quanto; 567 occasione; 677 the “S. S.” of either the Pope or a cardinal); the three-digit groups run roughly alphabetically. Unknown groups in the decodes fell from about 5,000 to about 800. 22 of the 28 sheets now read as sentences, with English (texts/R*_reading.md, _en.md), 0.80 of the tokens. Six are decoded, not read: R291 (open groups; Kiewning has a regest only and the sheet breaks off), R300 (its second page was never transcribed by DECODE and its digits are not separable on the scan), R301 (the continuation is not among the DECODE images), R302 (the Conti paragraph), R304 (the first paragraph, not yet checked on the scan throughout) and R313 (open groups). The read sheets keep scattered open groups, listed in each sheet’s log. Measured word by word on 24 October 2026, one of the 22, R303 (the end of R302), builds coherent sentences but reads at 94.3% of its words, under the 95% a complete reading needs.

The key also explains why every attack below failed. A null or a three-digit group shifts the phase of every two-digit code after it, so no fixed-width alignment can survive more than a few words. That is where the first-pass crib match stopped: the 8 after ho had been taken as the first digit of a two-digit code. The long repeated digit strings that section 04 reports are the nomenclator and syllable groups, written the same way every time.

01 The volume and the twenty-eight ciphertexts

Barb.lat. 6960 is on DigiVatLib at digi.vatlib.it/view/MSS_Barb.lat.6960, 194 pages, with an IIIF manifest. It is an old bitonal microfilm scan and the image service’s own info.json gives a maximum of 748 × 1088 pixels per page; anything larger the server returns is an upscale of that. At that size a figure is about ten pixels wide. DECODE, however, holds its own scans of the same leaves at 1491 × 2066 (four times the pixel area) behind its login. Anyone transcribing this manuscript should work from those, not from DigiVatLib’s.

DECODE’s records R286–R313 are the twenty-eight ciphered passages; R315 is the volume as a whole, described there as “mixed ciphertext and cleartext”. Each record’s note gives its page range in the same DigiVatLib pagination, so DECODE page n is canvas n. The ranges run from pp. 5–6 to pp. 192–193, and the volume’s own title describes the ciphers of Pallotto dal 4 agosto al 29 settembre 1629, though the records carry dates as late as 29 November.

The cipher sheets are written across the full width of the page in a dense unbroken stream of figures, about thirty lines to the page and some sixty-six figures to the line. The clear pages are written quite differently, in a narrow column on the right of the leaf with a wide margin. The two alternate through the volume.

Transcriptions of all twenty-eight were made for DECODE by volunteers in 2019 and 2020 and are downloadable as DOC_R*.txt. They record the figures one digit at a time, so the manuscript’s (decorative and irregular) grouping is lost. Measured, they come to 112,805 digits; the shortest is R308 at 854, the longest R306 at 9,332, and R286, the one worked in detail here, has 3,573.

02 The contents, printed in 1897

The relevant edition is Hans Kiewning (ed.), Nuntiaturberichte aus Deutschland nebst ergänzenden Aktenstücken. Vierte Abteilung: 17. Jahrhundert, Band 2: Nuntiatur des Pallotto 1628–1630, 2: 1629 (Berlin 1897). It is on archive.org as item 4-2_20200807, in full text.

Kiewning works from the copies received in Rome, ASV, Nunz. di Germania 119, and heads each ciphered despatch dechiffr. with the date the Roman office deciphered it: Nr. 153 is “Pallotto an Barberini, 1629 August 4, dechiffr. 22. August”. What he prints is therefore the contemporary decipherment. He gives the substantive passages in Italian verbatim and summarises the rest in German regesta, so the coverage is close to complete.

Two checks tie the register to the edition.

The 4 August despatch. The cipher on pp. 5–6 is headed Di Vienna 4 di Agosto, which is Nr. 153. The clear pages around it are the register’s own fair copy of the despatch, written apart from the cipher, and they run straight through the cipher sheet: p. 4 ends … non haver facoltà di accettar altro partito, che’l proposto in and p. 7 resumes nome del suo Rè. That sentence is Kiewning’s Nr. 153 word for word.

The enclosure. Page 13 is the paper Kiewning prints as Nr. 153, Beilage II, Propositione P. Valeriano per Sabran (his source: Bibl. Barber. LXIX 60 fol. 122). The manuscript reads, and the edition prints:

… il quale aggiunse di più, che fatta la pace, quando S. S.tà, come padre comune, proponga una lega et unione di questi eserciti, affine di abbatter il Turco commune nemico del nome christiano, che S. M.tà mandaria plenipotentiarii in Italia, acciò N. S.re potesse concludere così gran negotio, con che il demonio resteria ingannato e burlato.

What the ciphers hide, then, is the business of the Mantuan succession crisis in the second half of 1629: Pallotto’s attempts, working through the Capuchin P. Valeriano Magno and the imperial minister Eggenberg (Echembergh in the despatches), to mediate between the Emperor and the French envoy Sabran over Casale and Monferrato, the civil rather than armed possession of the duchies, the withdrawal of the French from Susa and the imperial troops from the Grisons, and, behind it, the fear that the real object was to humble Venice and restore imperial authority in Italy.

03 The attacks made before the key was known

The figures are numeric only, written without separators. Across all twenty-eight ciphertexts the digit frequencies are uneven (0 at 16.1 per cent, 4 at 4.8 per cent), and adjacent digits carry about 0.18 bits of mutual information, so the stream is structured. The attacks below did not find the structure.

The code width does not show. For a fixed-width numeric code one phase should stand out from the others. None does. Taking the twenty-eight ciphertexts together, the index of coincidence of two-digit codes is 0.01462 at phase 0 and 0.01466 at phase 1, and the digit distributions at even and odd positions agree to within 0.2 per cent at every digit. Three-digit codes behave the same way at all three phases.

A crib aligns for twenty-seven letters, then fails. With the plaintext of Nr. 153 in hand, the opening of R286 aligns exactly, as two-digit codes, for twenty-seven letters:

55 70 83 65 03 29 00 69 38 73 11 36 50 23 22 07 92 20 86 72 52 06 38 97 12 33 23
H  O  R  I  C  E  V  U  T  A  L  A  R  I  S  P  O  S  T  A  D  A  T  A  M  I  I

The assignments repeat: 38 occurs twice and is T both times, 23 occurs twice and is I both times, which by chance would happen about once in 180 tries. The twenty-eighth code conflicts, and from there nothing is consistent. A banded dynamic-programming aligner over the whole despatch, allowing two-digit codes plus one- and three-digit resynchronisation for transcription slips, and iterating the code table to convergence against a hand-corrected 3,359-letter crib, reached about 55 per cent code-to-letter consistency, above the 20 per cent a wrong alignment gives and below what a recovered key gives.

Ciphertext-only search fails. Fixed-multiset simulated annealing over the hundred two-digit codes, on 5,000 codes of text, scored by a space-free Italian five-gram model built for this from the repository’s clean Italian corpora (12.0 million characters), settles at −3.54 log-probability per character where real Italian of the period scores about −1.5. A syllabic model of the same codes (each two-digit group standing for one to four letters) was tested against the known plaintext and collapsed back to single letters for every well-attested code, so no syllable table was found.

The 55 per cent is not a partial key. A held-out test shows this. Learn the table on the first 55 per cent of R286 against the crib, freeze it, then align the remaining digits to the remaining crib and count confirmations (cases where the code was already in the table and agrees). Against eight controls that keep the table but shuffle the letters among the codes:

confirmations per letterDP score
key learned from the first half0.355−3266
shuffled controls (mean of eight)0.359−3313

No separation. The same test on a “two-digit codes plus nulls, no digit slips” model gives 0.440 against a control mean of 0.431, again no separation. The consistency figure is the aligner fitting the crib, and any partial key read off it would have been an artefact.

The positive control. Encipher the same crib with a random two-digit homophonic key, damage the digit stream with single-digit insertions and deletions at a chosen rate, and run the identical learner. The fraction of the true key it recovers:

digit error ratekey recovered
0 %100 / 100
0.5 %87 / 100
1.7 %7 / 100
4 %19 / 100

The method works on synthetic text; it needs a transcription accurate to better than about half a per cent of digits. Above roughly one per cent a two-digit key cannot be recovered from this much text even with the plaintext in hand, because every inserted or dropped digit flips the code phase for everything after it. That also explains why no phase preference shows in the statistics above: slips scattered through thirty lines randomise the phase across the corpus.

The run-length test. A crib aligner can fake a fit, because a fresh code may always be given a fresh letter. What cannot be faked is a long consistent run: inside one window a repeated code must carry the same letter and the mapping must stay a function, so a false run dies at its first repeat conflict. Scanning every pairing of digit offset against crib offset and extending each run while it stays consistent:

longest consistent run
real cipher against the plaintext of Nr. 15339 letters
control: plaintext letters shuffled (five runs)39, 39, 36, 38, 36
control: cipher reversed39
synthetic two-digit cipher, same text, 1.7 % digit noise207 letters
synthetic two-digit cipher, clean transcription2705 letters

The real material sits on its own chance baseline, while a two-digit cipher at the same transcription noise runs five times further. The tool works: on that noisy synthetic text this method recovers 81 of 100 key codes, where the dynamic-programming learner managed 7. And because chance runs here reach 36 to 39 letters, the twenty-seven-letter opening match quoted at the top of this page is below the baseline. It is not evidence, and the one-in-180 figure given for it above is wrong.

So transcription noise is no longer a sufficient explanation. On this evidence the cipher sheet is not a fixed-width two-digit encipherment of the text Kiewning prints as Nr. 153. The next test widened that to the whole volume.

The corpus search. The run-length test compares one ciphertext with one crib. This compares four ciphertexts with the entire edition at once, exactly. For a substitution, equal codes force equal letters, so take a window of the cipher, list every pair of positions inside it holding the same code, and require the plaintext to repeat at precisely those offsets. That condition is checked across all 1,161,303 letters of Kiewning in one pass, with a bitmask per gap.

Calibrated by planting the true plaintext in the corpus and enciphering it with a random key:

windows hitting the true positionfalse positives
synthetic, clean transcription253 / 2540
synthetic, 1.7 % digit noise16 / 2540

So even at the real noise level about six per cent of windows still give an exact hit, and the method never fires falsely. Applied to four of the ciphertexts at three code widths:

recordwidth 2width 3width 4
R2860 / 2661 / 2610 / 256
R2920 / 4600 / 4560 / 452
R3060 / 7102 / 7050 / 700
R3110 / 5101 / 5050 / 500

The four apparent width-three hits are artefacts: every one lands in the edition’s index of Roman numerals (XIUXXUXXUIXXUIIXXUIII…), a degenerate stretch of corpus where any repeat pattern matches. There are no genuine hits. Under the hypothesis, R286 alone should have produced of the order of eight, so the probability of seeing none is around e−8.

What was left. The ciphertexts are not fixed-width substitutions of anything printed in the volume, of Nr. 153 or of any other despatch. Two possibilities remained. Either the system has variable-length groups, which no fixed-width alignment can match however good the transcription (the absence of phase preference points the same way), or the sheets encipher text that is not in the edition: Kiewning prints the substance in Italian but renders the rest as German regesta, and that Italian exists nowhere in print. The key (section 00) later confirmed the first. Both were testable, the first with a variable-length version of the same tool, the second by weighing a ciphertext’s length against the part of its despatch the edition only summarises.

How good is the transcription? Checked on DECODE’s larger scan, line 1 of p. 5 differs from DECODE’s transcription in two digits out of seventy-four, both substitutions, with no length difference (the transcription has 93 where the scan shows 73, and 30 where it shows 38). Line 2 differs by a digit or two and possibly in length. So the transcription is close, no length error is demonstrated at the point where the alignment breaks, and the break there is not a transcription artefact. Two explanations therefore remain open and cannot be separated from this material: the transcription carries enough length errors elsewhere to defeat recovery, or the cipher is not a fixed-width substitution of this plaintext at all.

04 What the cipher is

Every test above is negative. This one is not, and it constrains the system. Count repeated digit substrings across all twenty-eight ciphertexts (112,805 digits) against an independent control with the same digit frequencies:

substring lengthrepeats in the cipherindependent controlratio
4105,170102,8651.0×
664,0839,5906.7×
828,51991313×
1012,109112,000×
169660—
24390—

Thirty-nine repeated twenty-four-digit strings where chance gives none. A homophonic cipher used with care would not do that, since homophones exist to break repetition, and this hand repeats long runs constantly. What repeats like this is a code: a nomenclator in which a given word or syllable is written the same way every time. The key (section 00) has both: homophones for the letters, and fixed groups for syllables, words and names. The fixed groups of varying length explain why every fixed-width attack above failed. Segmenting the corpus by BPE gives 266 token types over 44,816 tokens, averaging 2.5 digits and spread over lengths one to seven: syllable-scale, and not a fixed width.

Two further hypotheses were tested and fail. The repeat spectrum of the first despatch matches its crib’s at roughly one digit per letter (3,570 digits against 3,359 letters), which for twenty-one letters over ten digits would mean a polyphonic cipher; there the constraint reverses, equal letters forcing equal digits. Longest consistent letter-to-digit run: 17 letters, against shuffled controls of 18, 19 and 18, which is chance level. And since encipherment preserves order, a repeated BPE token should land on the same plaintext word each time; 16 token types of 74 do, against controls of 13, 12 and 13. Not significant.

One correction. Section 03 above reports no phase preference at widths two and three. I first offered that as evidence against fixed-width codes, and that was too strong: at the observed digit error rate a single despatch carries some sixty slips, each flipping the phase for the rest of its line, which would wash out a real phase signal by itself. The phase test is inconclusive. The calibrated corpus search is the sound version of the claim, and it stands.

05 The annotations in DECODE’s transcriptions

DECODE’s transcriptions are annotated, and I had only skimmed the head of the first one. Across the twenty-eight files there are about 640 <CLEARTEXT> marks, and in five records <PLAINTEXT> lines, DECODE’s notation for a decipherment written on the document itself.

recordclear-text marksdecipherment on the page
R29868
R29663
R30554In un lungo discorso
R30753
R29251
R30935Con l’ord(ina)rio passato intendo che fu scritto da S. M.tà à…
R30822restati più contenti della risolutione
R31314Nel particolare

Most of these despatches are mixed letters: short ciphered runs set inside clear Italian, clause by clause. R308 has, on the leaf:

Non sono · 369092022550852359029255082700852173166 2 8 · circa il particolare de · 6680650…
with, written above the figures: restati più contenti della risolutione

That is an exact crib with known boundaries, in the right register, and it is nowhere in Kiewning. It also explains why a crib from the printed edition could not match a record like this one: the ciphered passage is a single clause, and the edition prints the sense of the whole letter.

This crib did not fit either. Forty-one digits for thirty-four letters, with the figures and the gloss both checked against DECODE’s 1523 × 2088 scan. Searched exhaustively, under the deterministic constraint that section 04 implies and again allowing homophones:

modelresult
fixed width 1, 2 or 3no fit (41 is not divisible by 2)
deterministic letter code, groups of 1–2 digits0 solutions (exact)
deterministic letter code, groups of 1–3 digits0 solutions (exact)
2-digit groups, syllables of 1–3 letters0 solutions (exhaustive, 4.0 M nodes)
the same, homophones allowed0 solutions (exhaustive, 7.3 M nodes)

So either the decipherer’s gloss does not align exactly to that run (he may have glossed only part of it, or run on into the next), or the system is more complex than any of these. That is where the work stood on 20 September.

06 What stays open

  • The key is Biermann and Bosbach’s (2018), identified for 6960 by George Lasry. This project did not recover it. What remains open is the ~1,750 three-digit groups of 6960 that are missing from the 6956 key. Kiewning’s printed Italian could fill most of them by alignment, but that has not been done.
  • The twenty-seven-letter alignment at the head of R286 is not evidence: the chance baseline for consistent runs on this material is 36 to 39 letters, so it is a run of luck shorter than average.
  • Kiewning’s coverage is close to complete, and not literal: where he gives a German regest instead of the Italian, the exact wording is only in the cipher. Before the key was known this looked like the main difficulty. The volume’s clear pages do not contain the ciphered passage (pp. 3–4 and 7–9 run continuously through the cipher sheet), so the Italian of what pp. 5–6 encipher may survive only in ASV, Nunz. di Germania 119, which is not digitised. A crib drawn from the printed edition cannot fix those words.
  • The sibling volume, catalogue entry 236 (BAV Barb.lat. 6956, DECODE R215–R285 and R318), was deciphered by others with the Biermann and Bosbach key and has been removed from the catalogue.
  • Where the next attempt should start: extract every clear-text and decipherment annotation with the digit runs on either side, across all twenty-eight records. That yields hundreds of short ciphered clauses with their Italian context, and five with a contemporary decipherment attached. Check each gloss against the image before trusting its extent; the R308 gloss is the best-documented one and it still does not fit, which most likely means its extent is not what it looks like.

Full texts

The transcription and the decryption of every document on this page, so that the reading can be used and checked. Short files are shown here; all are in the repository.

R286, BAV, Barb.lat. 6960, pp. 5-6 (DigiVatLib pagination)

Ciphered despatches of Giovanni Battista Pallotto, nuncio to the Emperor, to Cardinal Francesco Barberini, Vienna, August-November 1629, on the Mantuan succession crisis: Pallotto's attempts, through P. Valeriano Magno and the imperial minister Eggenberg, to mediate between the Emperor and the French envoy Sabran over Casale, Susa and the Grisons passes. Kiewning (Nuntiaturberichte IV/2, 1897) prints the contemporary decipherments from the Rome-received copies. R286 (4 Aug 1629) is Kiewning Nr. 153, opening 'ho riceuto la risposta datami in scritto'.

Decryption

crib153.txt

Ho ricevuta la risposta datami in scritto all'instanza fatta da me col viglietto, del quale mandai copia con le passate.
Non solo persistono ne primi sensi, ma mostrano essersi affatto dimenticati di quello, che S. Mta, il principe d'Echembergh et altri ministri principali hanno piu e piu volte sentito da me nel tempo preciso, nel quale fu resa la lettera di S. Mta a N. Sre delle ragioni toccanti alla publica quiete, ma piu precisamente a gl'interessi della corona di Spagna et alla casa d'Austria, per le quali S. Bne giudico bene non passar gli uffitii nella detta lettera, e finalmente della diligenza, che volse far S. Bne per ritener la venuta del re di Francia in Italia con la missione di nuntio straordinario e della ragione, che lo ritardo, che furono gli uffitii e dichiarationi del conte di Monterey.
Della detta risposta mando l'annessa copia a V. S. Illma, ma perche penso di far riformarla, cosi nel punto accennato, come in alcune parole, che pure hanno del pungente, e penso dire di non haver mandata, sicome in effetto non mando, la risposta datami a N. Sre et a V. S. Illma.
La supplico reverentissimamente a fare, che non si sappia in conto veruno di detta risposta, accio non ne arguiscano poca sincerita.
Hieri mi conferi il padre Magno un discorso havuto col principe d'Echembergh intorno all'aggiustamento delle cose d'Italia, dal quale haveva ritratto, che si recederia dal rigore delle risposte e si sodisfaria S. Mta di condescender in alcuni punti, mostrando pero di farlo non ad instanza del re per trovarsi armato, per non parer di farlo per timore, ma si bene ad instanza di N. Sre come padre comune.
Quando venne il padre Magno da me, ero attualmente co'l signor di Sabran et havendomi il padre fatto dire, che desiderava parlarmi prima, che Sabran partisse, per li affari correnti, presi confidenza di far trattener Sabran in un altra camera e sentii il padre Magno.
Dopo di che havendolo richiamato in sua presenza Sabran e poi fatto chiamar anche monsignor vescovo di Mantova, accio il padre sentisse li lor sensi, incontinente ne da lui, ne da altri si potesse dubitare, che motivassero per mia instratione, ma piu tosto potesse far relatione de gli ufficii, che da me s'interponessero in conformita de sensi di questa corte, feci, che'l padre medesimo spiegasse, quanto haveva passato co'l principe, e li punti, ne quali pareva si riducesse il negotio, che sono annotati nel foglio alligato.
Rispose il signor di Sabran non haver facolta di accettar altro partito, che'l proposto in nome del suo re.
Che non ostante questo per il desiderio della pace, che sa esser nel medesimo re, havria preso ardire di spedirgli corriero et aspettarne risposta, quando le propositioni si fussero avvicinate alle cose possibili, ma non esser tali quelle, che si facevano, adducendo molte e potenti ragioni.
Nondimeno dopo longo discorso si cavo dal suo parlare, che esploraria la mente del re nel modo accennato, mentre il possesso civile e non armato de stati si dichiarasse intendersi, che l'imperatore l'acquistasse co'l mezzo di suo commissario non diffidente al duca di Mantova, e che non si facesse mutatione o alteratione alcuna nel governo civile, politico e militare delli due stati e nel trattamento, preeminenza e prerogative della persona del duca e sua famiglia.
Mentre la giustitia si dichiarasse doversi far per la via ordinaria e conforme alle leggi e constitutioni imperiali e la successione di detti stati si giudicasse secondo le ragioni, che competessero a ciascuna delle parti nel tempo della morte dell'ultimo duca Vincenzo.
E finalmente quanto al ritirar il Christianissimo le armi da Susa proporria al re uno de tre partiti, cioe: Che restando l'imperatore nella Retia, resterebbe nel medesimo tempo il re a Susa; o vero, che ritirandosi il re da questo, l'imperatore nel medesimo tempo si ritirasse dall'altro passo, o veramente, che restando l'imperatore ne detti passi per dignita dell'amministratione della giustitia et esecutione della sua sentenza si ritirasse il re da Susa ne piu vicini luoghi del suo stato, sinche fusse finita la causa, e l'imperatore fusse in termine da potersi ritirar da Grigioni.

Decryption: pallotto_bd2_1629.txt

Translation

R286_en.md

# R286 — Pallotto to Barberini, Vienna, 4 August 1629 ("A"): English translation

Translation (22 Oct 2026, Claude Opus 5.5) of `targets/pallotto1629/crib153.txt`, the hand-corrected Italian text of
Kiewning, *Nuntiaturberichte* IV/2 (1897), Nr. 153, printed from the Roman decipherment of this despatch (Arch. Vat.,
Nunz. di Germania 119 fol. 11-19). That text is R286's reading. The despatch continues on R287 (from "...acquired it
through a commissioner of his", in the last sentence of the fourth paragraph below), so the crib's last two sentences
overlap the start of R287 (see `R287_reading.md` and `R287_en.md`).

---

I have received the answer given me in writing to the request I made by the note, a copy of which I sent with the last
post. Not only do they persist in their first views, but they show that they have entirely forgotten what His Majesty,
Prince Eggenberg and other leading ministers heard from me again and again at the very time when His Majesty's letter
was delivered to Our Lord: the reasons concerning the public peace, but more precisely the interests of the crown of
Spain and of the House of Austria, for which His Beatitude judged it well not to make the representations asked for in
that letter; and finally the effort His Beatitude chose to make to hold back the coming of the King of France into Italy
by sending an extraordinary nuncio, and the reason that delayed it, which was the representations and declarations of
the Count of Monterrey.

I send Your Most Illustrious Lordship the enclosed copy of that answer; but because I mean to have it revised, both on the
point mentioned and in some words that also have a sting in them, I mean to say that I have not sent to Our Lord and to
Your Most Illustrious Lordship the answer given me, as indeed I do not send it. I most reverently beg you to see that
nothing whatever is known of this answer, so that they do not infer from it a want of sincerity.

Yesterday Father Magni told me of a conversation he had had with Prince Eggenberg about the settlement of Italian
affairs, from which he had gathered that they would draw back from the rigour of their answers and that His Majesty
would be content to give way on some points, while making it appear that he did so not at the instance of the King for
finding himself under arms, so as not to seem to do it out of fear, but rather at the instance of Our Lord as common
father.

When Father Magni came to me I was actually with Monsieur de Sabran, and as the Father had sent word that he wished to
speak to me before Sabran left, about current affairs, I took the liberty of having Sabran wait in another room and heard
Father Magni. After that, having called Sabran back into his presence and then also sent for Monsignor the Bishop of
Mantua, so that the Father might hear their views directly, and so that neither he nor anyone else could suspect that
they spoke on my instruction, but rather so that he could report on the representations I made in conformity with the
views of this court, I had the Father himself explain what had passed with the Prince and the points to which the
business seemed to be reduced, which are noted in the attached sheet. Monsieur de Sabran answered that he had no power
to accept any course other than the one proposed in his King's name. That notwithstanding this, out of the desire for
peace which he knows to be in the King himself, he would have ventured to send him a courier and wait for an answer, if
the proposals had come closer to what was possible; but that those being made were not such, and he gave many and
strong reasons. Nevertheless, after a long discussion, it was drawn from what he said that he would sound out the King's
mind in the manner indicated, provided that the civil and unarmed possession of the states were declared to mean that the
Emperor would acquire it through a commissioner of his who was not distrusted by the Duke of Mantua, and that no change
or alteration whatever would be made in the civil, political and military government of the two states, nor in the
treatment, pre-eminence and prerogatives of the person of the Duke and his family. Provided that justice were declared
to be done by the ordinary way and in conformity with the imperial laws and constitutions, and that the succession to
the said states were judged according to the rights belonging to each of the parties at the time of the death of the
last Duke Vincenzo. And finally, as to the Most Christian King's withdrawing his arms from Susa, he would propose to the
King one of three courses, namely: that if the Emperor remained in the Rhaetia (the Grisons), the King would remain at
Susa for the same time; or else that, if the King withdrew from the one, the Emperor would at the same time withdraw from
the other pass; or else that, if the Emperor remained in the said passes for the dignity of the administration of
justice and the execution of his sentence, the King would withdraw from Susa to the nearest places of his own state until
the case was finished and the Emperor was in a position to withdraw from the Grisons.

R287, BAV, Barb.lat. 6960, pp. 10-12 (DigiVatLib pagination), 4 Aug 1629, second half of Kiewning Nr. 153

End of the 4 August despatch: Sabran's courier to the King, Spanish suspicion that the French aim at the Empire, the reasons the Emperor's ministers give for war in Italy.

R287_reading.md

# R287 — Pallotto to Barberini, Vienna, 4 August 1629 (BAV, Barb.lat. 6960, pp. 3-6 of the cipher, DECODE R287; second part of the despatch begun on R286)

Reading, 22 Oct 2026 (reread batch b01, Claude Opus 5.5). What it rests on:

* **The whole sheet** is the continuation of Kiewning, *Nuntiaturberichte* IV/2 (1897), Nr. 153, pp. 280-282 (Arch.
  Vat., Nunz. di Germania 119), which prints the despatch in Italian from the Roman decipherment. The decode of the
  sheet with the 6956 key (`reread_b01/R287_dec.txt`) aligns with the print chunk by chunk at 0.13-0.6 cost per letter
  (`reread_b01/R287_align.txt`; the one 0.70 chunk is an OCR page break), so the reading is the print, its OCR corrected.
* **Where the figures say something else**, the reading follows the figures of this sheet (checked on the DECODE scans,
  `R287_corrections.tsv`), and the print's wording is given in `R287_reread_log.md`: "preeminenze e prerogative della
  persona e famiglia del duca", "dar gelosia", "luoghi accordati", "il padre Magno ha mandato", "per nascita suddito
  de' Spagnoli, per il domicilio di più di trent'anni in queste parti", "provisioni di guerra", "non [?deporrò] con tutto
  ciò promover".
* The docket on the last page (clear, written vertically): "Vienna / li 4 Agosto 16[29] / Con […] del Nun[…] / Decif.ta
  li 22 d[etto]" — deciphered on the 22nd, as Kiewning's heading says ("dechiffr. 22. August").

`[?word]` partly carried, `[…]` unread. The sheet begins in the middle of a word; the opening of the sentence is on
R286 and is supplied from Kiewning in angle brackets.

---

‹Nondimeno dopo longo discorso si cavò dal suo parlare che esploraria la mente del re nel modo accennato, mentre il
possesso civile e non armato de' stati si dichiarasse intendersi che l'imperatore l'acquista›sse co'l mezzo di suo
commissario non diffidente al duca di Mantova, e che non si facesse mutatione o alteratione alcuna nel governo civile,
politico e militare delli due stati e nel trattamento, preeminenze e prerogative della persona e famiglia del duca.
Mentre la giustitia si dichiarasse doversi far per la via ordinaria e conforme alle leggi e constitutioni imperiali, e la
successione di detti stati si giudicasse secondo le ragioni che competessero a ciascuna delle parti nel tempo della
morte dell'ultimo duca Vincenzo. E finalmente quanto al ritirar il Christianissimo le armi da Susa, proporria al re uno
de' tre partiti, cioè: che restando l'imperatore nella Retia, resterebbe nel medesimo tempo il re a Susa; o vero che,
ritirandosi il re da questo, l'imperatore nel medesimo tempo si ritirasse dall'altro passo; o veramente che, restando
l'imperatore ne' detti passi per dignità dell'amministratione della giustitia et esecutione della sua sentenza, si
ritirasse il re da Susa ne' più vicini luoghi del suo stato, sinché fusse finita la causa e l'imperatore fusse in termine
da potersi ritirar da' Grigioni; ma però che in tanto si demolissero così in Susa come in Avigliana tutte le
fortificationi e ripari che potessero impedire o ritardare il passo al Christianissimo, né fusse lecito in questo mentre
rinovarle o in altro modo dar gelosia al re di procurargli impedimento o difficoltà al passare, in caso che i Spagnoli
intentassero nuove violenze alli stati del duca; e Savoia lasciasse nel medesimo tempo Moncalvo e li altri luoghi
accordati al duca di Mantova; e che spediria al re, mentre venisse assicurato che, accettate simili propositioni, lo
fariano anche per parte dell'imperatore, e non succederia come delli altri partiti proposti per il passato al duca di
Mantova. Si restò in ultimo che 'l padre Magno desse in scritto li punti de' quali era restato con Echembergh, e che
Sabran daria in scritto le considerationi e le modificationi che se le offerivano.

In questa conformità questa mattina il padre Magno ha mandato a Sabran, monsignor di Mantova et a me il foglio, del quale
mando aggiunta la copia; ma Sabran non ha voluto dar altra risposta per l'incertitudine con che parla in fine di quelle
parole "Questo e quello", ma molto più per haver penetrato per via di persona confidentissima del padre Magno che detta
negotiatione si faccia dal detto padre ad instanza delli ambasciatori di Spagna e del principe d'Echembergh, non per
venirne ad alcuna conclusione, ma puramente per portar avanti et addormentar li Francesi e far trattenere il corriero o
gentilhuomo che Sabran sta in procinto di spedir al re con l'avviso del suo negotiato, o almeno acciò, spedendolo,
l'avviso della pendenza di trattatione d'accordo trattenga o intepidisca le provisioni di guerra, mentre questi dal canto
loro non cessano con ogni più esquisita diligenza di dar grandissima fretta alla loro armata et al duca di Fridlant.

E tanto più Sabran e li ministri de' collegati si persuadono questo avviso, quanto sanno il detto padre, per nascita
suddito de' Spagnoli, per il domicilio di più di trent'anni in queste parti et per havervi casato un suo fratello, esser
affatto imperiale.

Onde hoggi Sabran spedisce un gentilhuomo francese con li dispacci et avvisi del suo negotiato al re e [?Cardinal di
Richelieu], sollecitando al possibile le provisioni di guerra e l'avanzamento di militie in Italia.

Ma io non [?deporrò] con tutto ciò promover e facilitar quanto potrò detta trattatione, ancorché non habbia molta
speranza di buona conclusione, havendo non leggieri rincontri che 'l punto o pretesto della riputatione è quello che
manco li muove, ma che il motivo che li violenta a pigliar quest'occasione di guerra è per quietar l'imperio e liberarsi
dal pericolo di una general sollevatione, mandando fuori queste genti e 'l loro capo, il duca di Michelburgh; e quello che
gli alletta è la speranza di metter in gran posto la riputatione e dignità imperiale in Italia, ricuperar molti feudi
occupati (con uno de' quali so che han dato intentione al principe Savelli di sodisfare a' servitii e spese fatte per
l'imperatore), e mortificare et indebolir di modo la Republica di Venetia che non possa più macchinar contro gli
accrescimenti di casa d'Austria.
Translation

R287_en.md

# R287 — Pallotto to Barberini, Vienna, 4 August 1629: English translation

Translation of `R287_reading.md` (22 Oct 2026, Claude Opus 5.5), with the same holes. The text follows the Roman
decipherment printed by Kiewning (1897), Nr. 153, corrected to the figures of this sheet where they differ (see the
reading's header and the log). The opening words, in angle brackets, are on the preceding sheet R286.

---

‹Nevertheless, after a long discussion, it was drawn from what he said that he would sound out the King's mind in the
manner indicated, provided that the civil and unarmed possession of the states were declared to mean that the Emperor
would acquire it› through a commissioner of his who was not distrusted by the Duke of Mantua, and that no change or
alteration whatever would be made in the civil, political and military government of the two states, nor in the
treatment, pre-eminences and prerogatives of the person and family of the Duke. Provided that justice were declared to
be done by the ordinary way and in conformity with the imperial laws and constitutions, and that the succession to the
said states were judged according to the rights that belonged to each of the parties at the time of the death of the
last Duke Vincenzo. And finally, as to the Most Christian King's withdrawing his arms from Susa, he would propose to the
King one of three courses, namely: that if the Emperor remained in the Rhaetia (the Grisons), the King would remain at
Susa for the same time; or else that, if the King withdrew from the one, the Emperor would at the same time withdraw from
the other pass; or else that, if the Emperor remained in the said passes for the dignity of the administration of justice
and the execution of his sentence, the King would withdraw from Susa to the nearest places of his own state until the
case was finished and the Emperor was in a position to withdraw from the Grisons; but on condition that meanwhile all
the fortifications and defences that could hinder or delay the passage of the Most Christian King be demolished, both
at Susa and at Avigliana, and that it should not be lawful in the meantime to renew them, or in any other way to give
the King cause to suspect that he was being hindered or obstructed in crossing, in case the Spaniards attempted new
violence against the Duke's states; and that Savoy should at the same time leave Moncalvo and the other places agreed
upon to the Duke of Mantua; and that he would send to the King, provided he were assured that, once such proposals were
accepted, they would carry them out on the Emperor's side as well, and that it would not go as with the other courses
proposed in the past to the Duke of Mantua. It was settled in the end that Father Magni would put in writing the points
on which he had remained with Eggenberg, and that Sabran would put in writing the considerations and modifications that
occurred to him.

In accordance with this, this morning Father Magni sent Sabran, Monsignor of Mantua and me the sheet, a copy of which I
send attached. But Sabran would not give any further answer, because of the uncertainty with which it speaks at the end
in the words "this and that", but much more because he has found out, through a person in Father Magni's closest
confidence, that this negotiation is being conducted by the said Father at the instance of the Spanish ambassadors and
of Prince Eggenberg, not to bring it to any conclusion but purely to drag things on and lull the French to sleep, and to
have the courier or gentleman held back whom Sabran is on the point of sending to the King with the report of his
negotiation; or at least so that, if he does send him, the news that a treaty of agreement is pending may hold back or
cool the preparations for war, while they for their part do not cease, with every most exquisite diligence, to press
their army and the Duke of Friedland on with the greatest haste.

And Sabran and the ministers of the allies are the more convinced of this report as they know the said Father, a subject
of the Spaniards by birth, by his residence of more than thirty years in these parts and by having married a brother of
his here, to be entirely imperial.

So today Sabran is sending a French gentleman with the despatches and reports of his negotiation to the King and to
[?Cardinal Richelieu], urging as far as possible the preparations for war and the advance of troops into Italy.

But for all that I shall not [?give up] promoting and facilitating the said negotiation as far as I can, although I
have little hope of a good conclusion, having no slight indications that the point or pretext of reputation is what
moves them least, and that the motive which drives them to take this occasion for war is to quiet the Empire and free
themselves from the danger of a general uprising by sending these troops out, and their commander, the Duke of
Mecklenburg; and what entices them is the hope of setting the imperial reputation and dignity in Italy on a high footing,
of recovering many occupied fiefs (with one of which, I know, they have given Prince Savelli to understand that they
will reward the services rendered and expenses incurred for the Emperor), and of mortifying and weakening the Republic of
Venice in such a way that it can no longer plot against the growth of the House of Austria.

R288, BAV, Barb.lat. 6960, pp. 35-37 (DigiVatLib pagination), 4 Aug 1629, "B 24", Kiewning Nr. 154

The new Bohemian bishoprics and the Emperor's complaints about P. Valeriano Magni's writings on the Carolina and his alchemy and mines; Pallotto asks for orders.

Reading

R288_reading.md

# R288 — Pallotto to Barberini, Vienna, 4 August 1629, "B 24" (BAV, Barb.lat. 6960, three pages, DECODE R288)

Reading, 22 Oct 2026 (reread batch b01, Claude Opus 5.5). What it rests on:

* **Kiewning**, *Nuntiaturberichte* IV/2 (1897), Nr. 154 (the OCR prints "164"), pp. 284-285, Arch. Vat., Nunz. di
  Germania 119 fol. 19-20, "dechiffr. 22. August". Kiewning prints in Italian only two stretches: "tanto impertinenti
  ... e così era risoluto" and "e non vorrei che ... non lo faccia". There the decode aligns at 0.2-0.5 cost per letter
  (`reread_b01/R288_align.txt`) and the reading is the print, OCR corrected.
* **Everything else** (about three quarters of the sheet) is read here from the figures with the 6956 key
  (`reread_b01/R288_dec.txt`), each break checked on the DECODE scans (IMG_R288_I2495-I2497, `R288_corrections.tsv`),
  and from the clear words on the sheet: the heading "Nel particolar delli nuovi Vesc.di da erigersi in Boemia" above
  the first cipher line, and the clear sentence on p. 3 "Ho voluto tutto questo significar a V. S. Ill.ma acciò si
  compiaccia ordinarmi come doverò". Kiewning's German regest of these parts was used only as a check on the sense.
* Clear head: "Di Vienna 4 di Agosto 1629. All'Ill.mo sig. Card. Barberini [abbreviated]. B 24." P. 3 is numbered "25.".

`[?word]` partly carried, `[…]` unread, `[nnn]` an unidentified group.

---

Nel particolar delli nuovi vescovati da erigersi in Boemia vedo quanto V. S. Ill.ma mi comanda, et in quella conformità
ne procurarò l'effetto a suo tempo, non senza speranza di conseguirlo.

Diedi parte a S. M.tà come era giunto il breve et altre scritture per l'effettuatione del negotio de' beni ecclesiastici
di Boemia, pigliando occasione di farli efficace espressione dell'affetto paterno che S. S.tà [?gli] porta, della stima
che fa della pietà e zelo religioso [?della] S. M.tà e del gusto col quale incontra le occasioni di dargliene segni.
S. M.tà mostrò gradir quanto le dissi in questa parte, et uscì a dirmi se il P. F. Valeriano haveria parte in questo
negotio, e si mostrò molto disgustato di lui, dicendo che gli haveva [?fatto dar] alcune scritture toccanti alla
Carolina, tanto impertinenti come quelle del Francese; che prima di partir di qua le haveva promesso e giurato che non
haveria parlato parola in questo negotio, e poi haveva fatto il contrario. Mi confermò che di lui da molto tempo non
haveva buon concetto, che gli era andato sempre avanti con mille inventioni di miniere, di alchimia e di secreti per
allungar la vita e simili, e che tutti erano riusciti vani; che è cervello inquieto et inimico scoperto della
compagnia, e che però pensava non lo permettere in modo alcuno ne' suoi stati, e così era risoluto; ma che me lo diceva
in confidenza, e lo [?tenessi] in me stesso senza dirlo ad alcuno.

Dissi, quanto al punto della Carolina, non haver visto le scritture delle quali mi diceva S. M.tà (sì come non l'havevo
vedute, se bene il padre Magno mi haveva detto di farmele veder prima di darle), ma pregarla nondimeno a farle veder
attentamente, e se dicesse cosa rilevante, non [?perdessero] per far […] dal padre. Quanto al resto confermai a S. M.tà
che, [?quando] egli [?passasse] li termini del dovere, S. M.tà non si pigliasse pensiero, perché io, superior di lui, ci
provederei come conviene; e quando S. M.tà risolvesse di volerlo fuor de' suoi stati, io stesso trovarei il modo; ma
pregavo S. M.tà a sospender questa risolutione [?finché] fusse aggiustato il negotio de' beni ecclesiastici di Boemia,
per quale [332] [993] è molto [697] e necessario; poi haveria [249] a deliberar quello che [?havesse] giudicato più a
proposito. Con che S. M.tà mostrò di quietarsi.

Vede V. S. Ill.ma […] già la seconda volta che S. M.tà fa questo motivo, e non vorrei che, se questo S. M.tà mostra
dirlo in confidenza e con ordine di tenerlo secreto, pretendesse esser inteso, e come fare una tacita scusa di farlo per
se stesso, quando altri, a chi possa toccare, non lo faccia. Ho voluto tutto questo significar a V. S. Ill.ma, acciò si
compiaccia ordinarmi come doverò governarmi, se altra volta l'imperatore uscirà in questa materia, o vero si
determinasse a porre in esecutione il suo pensiero.
Translation

R288_en.md

# R288 — Pallotto to Barberini, Vienna, 4 August 1629 ("B 24"): English translation

Translation of `R288_reading.md` (22 Oct 2026, Claude Opus 5.5), with the same holes. Two passages follow the Roman
decipherment printed by Kiewning (1897), Nr. 154; the rest is read from the figures and the clear words on the sheet.

---

On the matter of the new bishoprics to be erected in Bohemia, I see what Your Most Illustrious Lordship commands me, and
in accordance with it I shall seek to bring it about in due time, not without hope of achieving it.

I informed His Majesty that the brief and other papers had arrived for carrying out the business of the church property
in Bohemia, taking the occasion to express to him forcefully the fatherly affection that His Holiness [?bears] him, the
esteem in which he holds [?His] Majesty's piety and religious zeal, and the pleasure with which he meets the occasions of
giving him signs of it. His Majesty showed that he welcomed what I told him on this point, and came out with asking me
whether Father Fra Valeriano would have a part in this business, and showed himself much displeased with him, saying
that he had [?had] some writings [?given] him concerning the Carolina, as impertinent as those of the Frenchman; that
before leaving here he had promised and sworn to him that he would not say a word in this business, and then had done
the opposite. He assured me that he had long had no good opinion of him, that he had always gone about with a thousand
inventions of mines, of alchemy and of secrets for prolonging life and the like, and that all had come to nothing; that
he is a restless brain and an open enemy of the Society [of Jesus], and that he therefore thought not to allow him in
any way in his states, and was so resolved; but that he told me this in confidence, and I should [?keep] it to myself
without telling anyone.

I said, as to the point of the Carolina, that I had not seen the writings His Majesty spoke of (as indeed I had not seen
them, although Father Magni had told me he would show them to me before handing them in), but begged her [Your Majesty]
nevertheless to have them read attentively, and if they said anything of weight, they should not [?lose] […] by the
Father. As to the rest, I assured His Majesty that, [?if] he [?overstepped] the bounds of what is proper, His Majesty
should not trouble himself, because I, as his superior, would see to it as is fitting; and if His Majesty resolved to
have him out of his states, I myself would find the way; but I begged His Majesty to suspend this resolution [?until]
the business of the church property of Bohemia was settled, for which [332] [993] is much [697] and necessary;
afterwards he would have [249] to decide what he [?had] judged most suitable. With this His Majesty showed himself
satisfied.

Your Most Illustrious Lordship sees […] already the second time that His Majesty raises this matter, and I should not
like it if, when His Majesty makes a show of saying this in confidence and with orders to keep it secret, he meant to be
understood, and as it were to make a tacit excuse for doing it himself, should others whom it may concern not do it. I
wished to tell Your Most Illustrious Lordship all this so that you may be pleased to instruct me how I am to conduct
myself if the Emperor comes out with this matter again, or if he resolved to put his intention into effect.

R289, BAV, Barb.lat. 6960, pp. 40-42 (DigiVatLib pagination), 10 Aug 1629, Kiewning Nr. 166 (first part)

Pallotto's audience with Ferdinand II over the Emperor's written answer that blamed the Pope's offices; the date of the Pope's audience for the imperial ambassador.

R289_reading.md

# R289 — Pallotto to Barberini, Vienna, 10 August 1629 (BAV, Barb.lat. 6960, pp. 28-29 of the register's numbering, four pages; DECODE R289; continued on R290)

Reading, 22 Oct 2026 (reread batch b01, Claude Opus 5.5). What it rests on:

* **The whole sheet** is printed in Italian by Kiewning, *Nuntiaturberichte* IV/2 (1897), Nr. 166 (heading Nr. 156,
  "dechiffr. 29. August"), pp. 286-288, Arch. Vat., Nunz. di Germania 119 fol. 22-27, from the Roman decipherment.
  The decode with the 6956 key (`reread_b01/R289_dec.txt`) aligns with the print chunk by chunk at 0.07-0.6 cost per
  letter (`reread_b01/R289_align.txt`; one chunk at 0.85, where the print's footnotes interrupt it), so the reading is
  the print, its OCR corrected.
* **Clear words on the sheet**, read on the DECODE scans (IMG_R289_I2498-I2501): "forse", "haver sentito che" (with
  "et" interlined in cipher, 79), "era", "ma più tosto, che", "maggiormente", "ma al fine restò", "far ogni maggiore e
  più". A clear gloss "In conformità della risposta" stands over the first cipher line.
* **Where the figures differ from the print**, the reading follows the figures of this sheet: "si aggiungeva" (print
  "si aggiunga"), "inviarlo ancora" (print "anche"), "che porta S. M.tà alla sede apostolica" (print "che portava").
  Details in `R289_reread_log.md`.
* Head (clear): "Di Vienna 10 di Agosto 1629. All'Ill.mo sig. Card. Barberini [abbreviated]"; a large "C" under the
  address; "28." and "29." are the page numbers.

The last words ("venne da me il giorno seguente") run on into R290 ("il Questembergh, al quale ...").

---

In conformità della risposta data al mio viglietto, della quale mandai copia con le passate, seppi che ministri
principali, e tra questi il signor principe d'Echembergh, dicevano a chi gli veniva occasione che l'aggiustamento di
queste turbulenze dependeva da N. S.re, in mano del quale stava il far ritirar d'Italia il re di Francia, sì come havria
anche potuto impedirne la venuta; ma che non solo non l'haveva fatto per se stesso, ma né anche stimolato dall'instanze
dell'imperatore, che glie ne haveva scritta lettera espressa.

Mi risolsi dunque di parlare a S. M.tà, e gli dissi che, quando come nuntio di N. S.re potessi credere di sodisfar al
mio debito con haver esposto in voce et in scritto a S. M.tà il desiderio di S. B.ne e co'l mandargli la risposta che a
S. M.tà era piaciuto farmi dare, di qualsivoglia maniera ch'ella si fusse, non stimavo di sodisfar all'obligo di buon
servitore di S. M.tà se non le mettevo in consideratione quello che circa detta risposta mi si offeriva; mentre,
persuadendomi che S. M.tà habbia desiderato di sodisfar alle instanze di S. S.tà, non potevo dubitare che fusse alieno
dalla sua intentione. Che al sentimento di non conseguir S. B.ne quello che ha procurato per publico bene della
christianità si aggiungeva quello di veder che S. M.tà, in vece di gradir quello che ha fatto in effetti e quel più che
ha voluto fare per il medesimo fine e per benefitio della casa d'Austria, gli dia colpa del contrario e si faccia in
scrittura che, restando nella publica cancellaria e dovendo andar per manus a principi e personaggi maggiori del mondo,
portasse testimonio ben differente da quello che è dovuto alla somma vigilanza e zelo verso la quiete della christianità
et al paterno cordiale affetto verso S. M.tà Ces.a e casa d'Austria, con che N. S.re haveva proceduto continuamente sin
dal principio di questi affari.

E qui spiegai e replicai a S. M.tà quello che gli havevo detto più volte: oltre alle diligenze interposte prima per mezzo
de' nuntii ordinarii e poi mediante la missione de' nuntii straordinarii in Spagna, in Italia e qua, haver S. S.tà voluto
inviarlo ancora al re di Francia, e non haverlo fatto per gli uffitii del conte di Monterey. La lettera di S. M.tà esser
stata resa a S. B.ne non prima che alli 27 di marzo, e così 21 giorni dopo che 'l re di Francia si era impadronito del
passo di Susa, e dopo le capitolationi stabilite con Savoia e Don Gonzalo, e dopo ritirato l'assedio di Casale. Haver in
detta occasione N. S.re messo in consideratione al principe Savelli che, trovandosi le cose in tale stato, qualsivoglia
trattatione sarebbe inutile, perché si risponderia esser il negotio aggiustato, et aspettandosi risposta di Spagna non si
poter in tanto metter a campo altra negotiatione; ma esser più considerabile che, facendosi uffitio in conformità della
lettera di S. M.tà e toccando a' Franzesi il punto che le armi dell'imperatore e del re Catt.co caleriano potentemente in
Italia, serviria solo per irritar il re di Francia e spingerlo in pregiuditio della publica quiete, ma precisamente de'
stati del re Catt.co e di casa d'Austria, a quello che di un re in persona co'l potente e numeroso esercito, come si
trovava appresso, et in Provenza sotto il duca di Ghisa, e con le armi de' collegati si poteva temere contro stati vicini
senza soldatesca, senza danari e senza vettovaglie, anzi in tempo di estrema carestia. Alli uffitii di N. S.re doversi
principalmente il non haver il Christianissimo applicato l'animo e le forze a nuovi acquisti in Italia, ancorché forse
stimolato da altri, e l'essersene ritornato in Francia e richiamatovi il Cardinal di Richelieu.

S. M.tà si mostrò così nuovo in questi punti che io ne restai attonito. Dopo di haver detto di haver scritta la lettera
prima che entrassero li Franzesi, et haver sentito che io non entravo nel tempo nel quale era stata scritta la lettera,
ma dicevo quando a N. S.re era stata data, che non fu prima che a' 27 di marzo, S. M.tà parlò come chi sospettasse che
al suo ambasciadore si fusse differita l'audienza e commodità di darla prima. Al che io soggiunsi supplicando S. M.tà a
non si persuader tal cosa, la quale volevo pur credere che non fusse stata scritta dal signor ambasciadore, ma più tosto
che l'havesse data a suo commodo; e che, quando egli non havesse aspettata l'audienza ordinaria, non gli saria stata
negata l'audienza straordinaria ogni volta che l'havesse domandata. In conclusione dichiarò S. M.tà di gradir l'uffitio
che io havevo passato seco (poiché per non impegnar N. S.re in qualsivoglia evento dichiarai sempre non parlar come
nuntio), et esser sua intentione che si desse ogni sodisfattione possibile a S. S.tà, e mi disse che ne parlassi col
principe d'Echembergh.

Andai da S. E. l'istesso giorno, e seco mi diffusi maggiormente e ponderai di più le parole "sequitur etc." e "non
adsequitur etc.", la prima delle quali, massime dopo le precedenti, pareva che non solo toccasse l'efficacia, ma la
sincerità ancora delli uffitii di N. S.re, e la seconda qualificasse per men giustificata e quasi spropositata la domanda
di S. S.tà. Veramente restai confuso quando m'accorsi che anche al signor principe arrivavano come nuove le cose sudette,
le quali da me più e più volte gli erano state dette; ma al fine restò persuaso dall'evidenza di quello che io li
dicevo, e mi fece con grand'esaggeratione espressione della somma riverenza et osservanza che porta S. M.tà alla sede
apostolica et a N. S.re, e che è tanto alieno dalla sua intentione il far o dir cosa della quale possi restar offeso,
quanto è suo particolarissimo desiderio di far ogni maggiore e più viva dimostratione di ossequio e rispetto verso quella
santa sede. Concluse che manderia da me il Questembergh, che haveva fatta la risposta, acciò sentissi quello che io le
dicessi e potesse accomodarla. In questa conformità venne da me il giorno seguente
Translation

R289_en.md

# R289 — Pallotto to Barberini, Vienna, 10 August 1629: English translation

Translation of `R289_reading.md` (22 Oct 2026, Claude Opus 5.5). The text follows the Roman decipherment printed by
Kiewning (1897), Nr. 166, corrected to the figures of this sheet in three places; the clear words on the sheet agree with
it. The despatch continues on R290.

---

In accordance with the answer given to my note, of which I sent a copy with the last post, I learned that leading
ministers, and among them Prince Eggenberg, were saying to anyone who gave them occasion that the settlement of these
troubles depended on Our Lord, in whose hands it lay to make the King of France withdraw from Italy, just as he could
also have prevented his coming; but that he had not only not done it of his own accord, but not even when urged by the
instances of the Emperor, who had written him an express letter about it.

I therefore resolved to speak to His Majesty, and told him that, even if as nuncio of Our Lord I could believe I had
discharged my duty by having set out to His Majesty by word of mouth and in writing His Holiness's wish, and by sending
him the answer His Majesty had been pleased to have given me, of whatever kind it might be, I did not consider that I
was discharging the obligation of a good servant of His Majesty unless I put before him what occurred to me about that
answer; for, persuading myself that His Majesty has wished to satisfy His Holiness's instances, I could not doubt that it
was foreign to his intention. That to His Holiness's regret at not obtaining what he has sought for the public good of
Christendom there was added that of seeing His Majesty, instead of appreciating what he has actually done and the more
he has wished to do for the same end and for the benefit of the House of Austria, blame him for the contrary, and put it
in writing that, remaining in the public chancery and having to pass through the hands of the greatest princes and
personages in the world, would bear witness very different from what is due to the supreme vigilance and zeal for the
peace of Christendom, and to the fatherly and cordial affection towards His Imperial Majesty and the House of Austria,
with which Our Lord had acted continually from the beginning of these affairs.

And here I explained and repeated to His Majesty what I had told him several times: that besides the efforts made first
through the ordinary nuncios and then by sending extraordinary nuncios to Spain, to Italy and here, His Holiness had
wished to send him to the King of France as well, and had not done so because of the representations of the Count of
Monterrey. That His Majesty's letter was delivered to His Holiness no earlier than 27 March, and thus twenty-one days
after the King of France had seized the pass of Susa, and after the capitulations concluded with Savoy and Don Gonzalo,
and after the siege of Casale had been lifted. That Our Lord had on that occasion pointed out to Prince Savelli that,
things being in that state, any negotiation would be useless, because the answer would be that the business was
settled, and, an answer from Spain being awaited, no other negotiation could be set on foot meanwhile; but, more
important, that if representations were made in accordance with His Majesty's letter, touching the French on the point
that the arms of the Emperor and of the Catholic King would descend in force upon Italy, it would only serve to irritate
the King of France and push him, to the prejudice of the public peace, and precisely of the states of the Catholic King
and of the House of Austria, to what might be feared against neighbouring states from a king in person with the powerful
and numerous army he had close at hand, and in Provence under the Duke of Guise, and with the arms of the allies, without
soldiers, without money and without provisions, indeed in a time of extreme famine. That to Our Lord's representations
was chiefly owed the fact that the Most Christian King had not turned his mind and forces to new acquisitions in Italy,
although perhaps urged on by others, and that he had returned to France and recalled Cardinal Richelieu there.

His Majesty showed himself so new to these points that I was astonished. After saying that he had written the letter
before the French entered, and hearing that I was not concerned with the time the letter was written but was saying
when it was delivered to Our Lord, which was not before 27 March, His Majesty spoke like one who suspected that his
ambassador had been put off from his audience and the opportunity to deliver it earlier. To which I added, begging His
Majesty not to persuade himself of any such thing, which I would rather believe had not been written by the Lord
Ambassador, but rather that he had delivered it at his own convenience; and that, if he had not waited for the ordinary
audience, an extraordinary audience would not have been refused him whenever he had asked for one. In conclusion His
Majesty declared that he welcomed the representation I had made to him (for, so as not to commit Our Lord in any event,
I always declared that I was not speaking as nuncio), and that it was his intention that every possible satisfaction be
given to His Holiness, and he told me to speak of it with Prince Eggenberg.

I went to His Excellency the same day, and with him I enlarged further and weighed more closely the words "sequitur
etc." and "non adsequitur etc.", the first of which, especially after what preceded, seemed to touch not only the
efficacy but also the sincerity of Our Lord's representations, and the second to qualify His Holiness's request as less
justified and almost unreasonable. Truly I was confounded when I realised that to the Lord Prince too the things
mentioned above came as news, though I had told them to him again and again; but in the end he was persuaded by the
evidence of what I told him, and expressed to me with great emphasis the supreme reverence and respect that His Majesty
bears the Apostolic See and Our Lord, and that it is as far from his intention to do or say anything by which he might be
offended as it is his most particular wish to give every greater and livelier demonstration of deference and respect
towards that Holy See. He concluded that he would send me Questenberg, who had drafted the answer, so that he might hear
what I had to say to him and could amend it. In accordance with this there came to me on the following day

R290, BAV, Barb.lat. 6960, pp. 46-47 (DigiVatLib pagination), 10 Aug 1629, Kiewning Nr. 166 (second part)

Questenberg and the revised imperial answer; Eggenberg on deciding the Mantuan case by justice and the Emperor's coronation in Bologna or Ferrara; Sabran's departure, Wallenstein's gout, Magdeburg, Saxony and the Edict of Restitution.
